#161. Sullivan: What the 2026 midterms could mean for energy investors

Don't expect huge near-term moves in energy stocks off the results of the midterms. But there are some investing implications over the longer-term. Let me break it down.

Yes, it's entirely possible that one or both houses of Congress will shift to Democratic control. That's not my opinion. It's what many of the research reports, polls, and prediction markets are suggesting as of this writing.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm elections

For argument's sake, let's say that this time the polls are correct. The election ends with a "blue trickle," and Democrats win out with a slight edge in the House, Senate, or both. It won't be enough to meaningfully change, well, anything. If the election ends with just a slight lean to the Democratic side, it won't be enough for a veto-proof majority. Candidates can specify policies — like halting the growth of data centers — all they want, but there's a chasm between being elected and passing new laws.

Research I've read and conversations we've had on CNBC suggest that the next two years in Washington, D.C., will be flush with finger-pointing, investigations into the president and his family, and posturing ahead of the far-more-important 2028 presidential election.

S&P Global doesn't exactly agree with me, saying that Democratic control of either chamber would bring "tougher oversight of agency decisions" and could impact things such as tax incentives and "other policies underpinning long-term energy investments."

A Democratic Congress could be good for offshore wind, an energy source the president seems to hate with the white-hot passion of the sun. Speaking of the sun, a shift blue could also be positive for the continued growth of solar, perhaps via new tax incentives. Though both are growing rapidly.

If the Republicans maintain control over one or both houses, we keep doing more of the same, albeit with more urgency. S&P Global adds that "a continued Republican majority would give the administration more room to pursue expanded domestic production, faster permitting and environmental deregulation, though narrow margins could limit major legislation."

Any change in control of Congress is also unlikely to make a big difference around the Iran conflict. Once any new Congress begins in January, things will have either ramped up, stayed the same, or cooled down. A new Congress could try to use its authority under the War Powers Act. The team at Piper Sandler agrees, writing that "elections matter, but will likely not change the direction of things" as Trump focuses more on his own legacy. That's a polite way of saying that the President is going to do largely what he wants, Congress be darned.

On the state level, the midterms may matter a little bit more, particularly as they relate to energy-hungry data centers. In Texas, a change in the statehouse could bring a change in sentiment around data centers. Candidate Gina Hinojosa has a policy to "end data center madness" and supports a moratorium on new builds. Amy Acton also supports a "conditional moratorium" on new data center builds in Ohio. Vivek Ramaswamy is also calling for conditions on new data center construction.

The issue is a big deal in many states. Yet even if those candidates, or candidates with similar views on big new energy projects, win, they'd still have to enact meaningful change. Which could happen, of course. So let's say it does happen. New governors enter office and use their power to throttle or even stop new projects. Even if some of the big new projects are stalled or stopped, power demand, at a minimum, would stay at current levels. Which means demand for power production inputs such as natural gas, nuclear, and yes, even coal, would remain at current levels.

As far as power-hungry utility stocks go, Bank of America agrees with my election take above - mostly. Its team writes that it thinks the "2026 cycle matters for utility equities in far fewer states than the headline count suggests," but that committee appointments in each state will matter. The BofA team says that, regarding the election and utility stocks, five states matter most: Georgia, Ohio, Oregon, Pennsylvania, and Texas.

Texas is the "most consequential," as state environmental regulators have halted data center permits. That state's Commission on Environmental Quality (TCEQ) has actually stopped electronic permitting to make sure any permit is unrelated to data centers. To my point, that's already known information. It matters, but markets are already aware of it and have priced it in. Regardless, if you are investing in anything related to data centers, it wouldn't hurt to pay attention to Texas' election most of all.

There's a lot here. So take a breath, step back, and consider where we are right now.

The Strategic Petroleum Reserve is at a 40-year low.

We have two wars in some of the most important energy regions in the world.

The backlog for data centers - and thus the power needed to run them - is years long. Even if a number of them end up canceled, others will be built.

These days, it's hard to be certain of anything. But I am certain that overall energy demand isn't going down in America anytime soon. The only way to go is up. It's just a question of how much farther up.

That's my opinion. It could be wrong. And since I love hearing from you, I put out a poll on both X and LinkedIn. The results suggest you disagree with me.

Note → I am fully aware that the results of any poll on X or LinkedIn will be skewed by my type and makeup of followers on each platform - who lean heavily into both business, money and energy.

So if the elections won't matter in the near term for energy, what does?

Glad you asked.  Here's my ranking of what will matter for energy in the next year:

(Disagree?  Hit me up on X or LinkedIn and let me know why)

An end to either the Iran conflict or Russia's war on Ukraine would be massively positive for the world and send oil, natural gas, and refined products lower relatively quickly. Let's all hope for peace.

Lower interest rates would help many of the beaten-up stocks that rely on debt and consumer lending to prosper. Think residential solar panels.

The macro economy is going to do what it will, regardless of the U.S. elections, and American tax policy is largely unchangeable for the next couple of years.

Permitting reform is, of course, one factor that could be impacted by the election outcome, though it's unlikely to happen in the near term.

No, my friends, whatever the outcome of America's elections, it's safe to say the real political attention will be on Trump, more Trump, Trump's family, even more Trump, and then looking ahead to 2028's presidential vote.

If we could harness political infighting into some sort of power source, it may be better than nuclear!

I'm leaving for Europe this week to continue our five-year coverage of Europe's slow-rolling energy crisis. And while the word "crisis" is way overused in the media, I don't think it is here. Natural gas import prices are soaring. Storage levels are below recent averages. Interest rates are rising, and the public is already rage-filled about higher energy prices. It's a toxic combo. And I'll be in Europe later this week to talk about it.

First, from a macro markets perspective, I remain wary of tech and the stock market right now. As I posted on October 14, the markets "feel fragile."

Since writing that, the Nasdaq is down over 5%. A.I. isn't in a bubble, but not all companies are going to prosper. Some may go away. Watch the debt markets, particularly some A.I.-related high-yield (aka "junk") bonds. The State Street High Yield (JNK) ETF is prominently on my screens these days. Get to know the term "leveraged ETF," because those are in focus these days. The selling in many of these products can lead to selling in other products and stocks. If we get a slowdown in selling in those, some of the big tech stocks that have been selling off might stabilize.

Turning more specifically to why you are here: energy.

Earnings are (almost) behind us. ExxonMobil (XOM) and Chevron (CVX) posted big numbers last week. Marathon Petroleum (MPC) just reported, and those numbers were even bigger. The company earned $26.59 per share on $11.7 billion in EBITDA. That compares to EPS of $3.01 on earnings of $3.2 billion EBITDA one year ago.

ConocoPhillips (COP) caps things off later this week.

What now is that the world waits, watches, and focuses on Iran, the Strait of Hormuz, and Russia. What happens with those will determine the path of prices for oil and much more. Peace, and prices go down. New attacks, prices go higher. Higher prices mean many big-cap oil and gas stocks keep going higher. If Russia continues its brutal war against Ukraine, diesel fuel and jet fuel prices are going to remain volatile.

My take → Keep an eye on any headlines related to Russia selling diesel fuel to the United States. There is one ship on the way (see the RBI below), and let's see if more follow.

The continued risk around Iran and Russia pushed Goldman Sachs to raise its oil price forecast for the rest of this year. The firm is taking Brent crude to $105 and WTI crude to $94, and raising its average targets for next year to $88 for Brent (from $80) and $78 for WTI (from $73). Let's see if others follow.

While earnings have U.S. analysts swamped, Goldman's European team isn't too busy to update its "European Conviction List" of stocks. Some of the names it likes most are energy-related, including German power producer RWE (RWE-DE) and the better-known Siemens Energy (ENR-DE).

Concerns about AI, power, and rising interest rates have recently hit both stocks. RWE is down 4% this quarter, with Siemens Energy falling over 10%. Goldman remains bullish. The firm has a €212 price target on Siemens Energy and a €75 target on power producer RWE. That implies about a 30% return on RWE and a huge 70% upside on Siemens Energy.

Europe may have a world of energy problems, but that doesn't mean some of its stocks can't make you money. For more, tune in to my live CNBC coverage from Italy on Thursday, November 5.

We recently spoke with Toby Rice, CEO of natural gas giant EQT (EQT). His company just made a massive deal to buy Range Resources (RRC). We spoke with Toby about the deal, energy markets, and the importance of U.S. gas to Europe.

Another important interview on energy and AI this week was with Brian Nowak of Morgan Stanley. Brian focuses on tech firms such as Amazon, but his comments about some of the AI power players - the names we mention above - are worth a listen.

This week's Inside Line is with Christoph Halser, Gas & LNG analyst at Rystad Energy.

Could the one ship carrying Russian diesel fuel bound for America… actually skip America? It might.

The vessel we've had a close eye on for a week is moving slowly in the Atlantic Ocean. So slowly that some are now wondering if the ship will actually make it to New York. Could the destination change mid-voyage? It happens with ships all the time. A new buyer steps in to buy the cargo, and the ship changes course.

It does make you wonder… what American company would buy a load of Russian diesel right now? Putting money into Putin's pocket is not the PR that any company I know would want, but stranger things have happened.

The ship is supposed to dock in New York on November 7. Following the course of this ship right now is definitely random, but interesting.

OpenAI deepens Abu Dhabi foothold as Sam Altman inks deals spanning energy, government and investment

World's energy supply buffers have been eroded, ADNOC CEO says (Reuters)

U.S. Pushes to Restore Russian Gas Sales to Europe Via Ukraine (WSJ)

#159. 'I live with a ghost' - inside the haunted homes attracting bargain hunters

The previous occupant of Ryua's apartment, pictured here, sadly took their own life

From the moment Ryua first walked into her rental apartment in Tokyo she believed it to be haunted.

"When I visited the place for a viewing there was a man standing there, glaring at me," she says.

"Since the estate agent couldn't see him, I realised he must be a ghost."

While someone more squeamish would probably have quickly left the flat never to return, Ryua chose to go ahead and move in.

"I'm a bit of a contrarian, so I decided I wanted to become friends with this glaring male ghost," adds Ryua, who works as an aidoru, a young entertainer, and who doesn't wish to share her surname.

Four years later, and still in the same apartment, she says she continues to experience paranormal activity on a daily basis. She says she hears endless footsteps and the ghost talking.

Ryua says that she shares her Tokyo apartment with a ghost

Although Japan has increasingly marked Halloween, external in recent years, many Japanese people take ghosts seriously all year round. The country has a word, "yuurei", which means a lingering, troubled spirit.

These are the ghosts of people who died in violent or tragic circumstances - those who were murdered, killed themselves, or simply were lonely and died alone. If they died at home, then the belief is that their yuurei won't go away.

For house hunters who are not superstitious or squeamish it means there are big bargains to be found. There is a whole category of dwellings called jiko bukken, which means "stigmatised" or "incident" properties.

Under guidelines issued by the government in 2021, Japanese estate agents must disclose suicide, murder, death by fire, and any death where the body was not discovered for an extended period of time.

For rental properties this information must be disclosed for three years, and it must be revealed indefinitely for any property offered for sale.

By contrast, in the UK there is no legal requirement, external to reveal that a person died in a property. And in the US, it differs from state to state., external For example, in California you have to reveal if a person has died in the property in the past three years, while in Arizona and Oregon you do not.

Ryua was told that a man had killed himself in her apartment. It meant that she gets an almost 30% discount on her rent on her tiny, 25 sq m bedsit in the fashionable ward of Setagaya. She says she pays 45,000 yen ($285; Â£213) a month, instead of what should have been 62,000 yen.

While a house where there had been a murder or suicide would probably be harder to sell or rent out anywhere in the world, Tokyo estate agent Erik Oskamp says that Japanese people are particularly superstitious and averse to stigmatised properties.

"[Even] an apartment next to a cemetery will be difficult to rent out, especially if you can see the tombs from the kitchen," explains Oskamp. "The worst thing is if a suicide has happened in the apartment. Even if somebody has jumped from the roof, many renters will move out. Other issues are a serious crime or a lonely death."

The latter issue, that of a person dying alone, would not generally affect the price of a property in, say, the UK or the US.

But in rural Kagoshima, in Japan's southwest, it enabled Diane Tincher to buy a large three-bedroom house for about half of what would have otherwise been the asking price.

The 69-year-old American has lived in Japan for nearly 40 years, working as a tour guide. She says that the estate agent had been straight with her â there was a heavy discount as an elderly lady had died alone, thereby stigmatising the property.

Tincher paid 20m yen for the house, in a location where similar properties usually sell for up to 45m yen.

"I don't believe in ghosts. But I know that the Japanese are very superstitious," she says. "If there is a ghost I'm thankful to her every day... to live in such a splendid and affordable home."

Diane Tincher says she got a 50% reduction on her home in rural Japan

Researching the house, Tincher had found it on a website called Oshimaland, which lists jiko bukken properties, giving details of the events that occurred. It was launched in 2005 covering Tokyo, before expanding across Japan and then internationally in 2011.

It is crowdsourced, meaning that members of the public add the homes and their details.

Diane Tincher got a huge discount on her home in rural Japan

Teru Oshima, the founder of Oshimaland, says that in the past such properties were "not very attractive options" in Japan.

Yet as house prices have surged in the country in recent years, as well as a big increase in the wider cost of living, one 2025 survey found that more than half of Japanese people would now rent a jiko bukken if it was cheap., external

Back in Setagaya, Ryua says she is happy living with her ghostly flatmate. "I believe that ghosts were once living human beings, so they deserve our respect," she says.

#157. AI-generated 'slopaganda' in political ads puts internet platforms on alert

The midterm election cycle has been dominated by "slopaganda," or campaign ads featuring AI-generated clips and images, creating another challenge for Instagram, YouTube and X as they attempt to monitor their platforms.

The term slopaganda dates back to early last year, when a group of experts on artificial intelligence used it in a paper to describe a new phenomenon combining generative AI slop with propaganda and misinformation.

There's no comprehensive federal law governing the use or disclosure of AI in political ads. And while there are some 30 state laws with different requirements about AI use and disclosure, candidates don't always abide by them. On the leading internet sites run by Meta, Google and Elon Musk's SpaceX, users run into AI-generated ads and AI-generated posts shared by politicians, alongside authentic videos and clips.

More than $187 million has been spent on nearly 300 ads generated or enhanced by AI, according to an analysis released last week by the Wesleyan Media Project. That's almost certainly an undercount, the organization says, because so many ads don't contain disclaimers and because determining AI use is difficult in some instances. Over 90% of the spending came from Republican candidates, a Republican party committee or a Republican-leaning group, the report found, and most don't include disclosures.

CNBC found a number of AI-generated ads running online without disclosures, including a fake video of New York Democratic Gov. Kathy Hochul that was posted by her opponent, Republican challenger Bruce Blakeman.

In Texas, Republican senatorial candidate Ken Paxton created an AI-generated video showing Democratic opponent James Talarico making controversial statements, with a three-second disclosure flashing in the middle. It's being shared as organic content on YouTube and X by Paxton's campaign. "Organic content" refers to free, unpaid posts shared on a profile or channel.

The Blakeman and Paxton campaigns didn't immediately respond to requests for comment.

YouTube told CNBC that the company applies "prominent labels to realistic AI content."

"For organic content on YouTube, political candidates can request removal where their face appears to be altered or generated by AI," the company said. "For advertisers, we require disclosure when an election ad contains synthetic or digitally altered people or events to provide viewers with transparency."

YouTube parent Google also requires political ads to complete its election ads verification process and include "paid for by" disclosures.

X says advertisers must comply with applicable laws, putting the responsibility on the advertiser, not the platform. The company didn't respond to CNBC's request for comment.

Meta referred to its guidelines on political ads, including restrictions on their use in the week prior to the election. The company also mandates "paid for by" labels and maintains an ad library of political ads. In June, the company ramped up its labeling, adding a new "about this ad" tool for ads that Meta detects have been created with third-party AI tools.

One risk the tech platforms face with generative AI is being asked to create misleading images and videos. The Brennan Center for Justice tested ChatGPT, Gemini, Grok, Claude, Perplexity and DeepSeek for their willingness to facilitate deceptive content. The study from August found that many models may avoid generating content around familiar conspiracies, but can generally be manipulated into creating misleading material.

A retest of those platforms in October found that ChatGPT had made progress. It had started redirecting prompts for harmful election images into illustrations so they look like comics, or refusing to take action on them altogether.

A number of national politicians are trying to address the issue.

The Protect Elections from Deceptive AI Act, backed by senators from both parties, would prohibit knowingly distributing materially deceptive AI-generated video or audio about federal candidates. Other bills are being led by Democrats in Congress, including the AI Ads Act, which is looking to expand current bans on campaign misrepresentation to include generative AI content.

None of the proposed bills have advanced out of committee.

#156. How video game remakes tap the power of nostalgia

The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time is often rated as one of the greatest video games

The moment she saw footage from the remake of The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time, Kacey Doran picked up her phone. She had to tell her sister.

"Have you seen this?" messaged Doran, excitedly.

Nearly 30 years ago, a 10-year-old Doran had sat in front of her family's TV, reading out the dialogue on screen to her sister, a year younger, as they inched their way through the original Nintendo 64 game's sprawling, puzzle-filled dungeons. Ocarina's fantasy-like story, and beautiful music, enthralled them both.

It was also the first game Doran ever completed. Now, countless memories rushed back to her as she devoured clips of the fully redesigned version, due to arrive on the Switch 2 later this year.

Doran's sister messaged back: "I'm not tearing upâ¦ YOU'RE tearing up."

News of a video game remake bought back happy memories for Kacey Doran

This is the power of nostalgia â and a perfect example of how video games can trigger it. Doran now teaches an esports course at Rowan University in New Jersey. She says the original Ocarina of Time, released in 1998, had a "magical" quality that she hopes to rediscover, and also share with her sister's children.

The video games industry has long leaned on remakes. From Mario Kart to the Final Fantasy series, or even Pac-Man, some games just keep coming back in updated forms, as new generations of gamers emerge and as computer hardware improves, enabling more detailed graphics and increasingly expansive game worlds.

While the emotional hook may be the most significant draw, putting out a remake can be a double-edged sword. Some gamers are very protective of their favourite titles and new versions don't always meet certain fans' astronomically high expectations.

Nostalgia is an important but mixed emotion, says Tim Wulf, at the Centre for Advanced Internet Studies in Bochum, Germany. It has positive aspects but also a kind of sadness, too. Happiness in remembering things that were important to us, and also an awareness that that time is now gone.

"It's a social emotion because memories that induce nostalgia for us are connected to other people," he explains, adding that nostalgia can boost our self-esteem while highlighting key social bonds.

Video games, says Wulf, are notoriously good at triggering nostalgia because gamers can "jump into the same environment". In other words, games are like time machines for our emotions, and returning to fondly remembered places can feel stabilising or reassuring in an ever-changing world.

Doran, for one, notes that when you return to a favourite old game, there's something satisfying about deploying previously learned skills. You know what to do here, you're winning again. Just like the old days.

Video games are good at triggering nostalgic feelings says Tim Wulf

Putting out remasters and remakes has been a "really successful" strategy for the games industry, says Piers Harding-Rolls, head of games research at Ampere Analysis.

"Tying in with established audiences and building on existing content is quite a safe route for a lot of companies," he adds. In many cases, it is cheaper to update an existing game rather than create a new title from scratch.

Among other remakes that have found big audiences recently are those of Resident Evil 4 and Assassin's Creed Black Flag.

But Ocarina of Time perhaps stands out because, ever since its original release, it has been considered by many as one of the greatest games ever made. "The interest from all the Gen Zs that I work with is really high," says Harding-Rolls. The legend of The Legend of Zelda, as it were.

Since 2007 there have been 14 main releases of Assassin's Creed

Clearly, nostalgia is not the only driver of interest in the remake, and today's audiences will expect a game that plays to today's standards.

Games companies must keep innovating when linking contemporary audiences to the past, says Wulf, in order satisfy their desire for fresh experiences. And although Wulf himself was a big fan of the original, he has no personal interest in the Ocarina of Time remake. "I want a new game â I don't want to play an old game," he says. Nostalgia isn't always enough for some.

Plus, critics often highlight a tension between originals and remakes. Which is the definitive version? In 2021, Square Enix released the bizarrely titled Nier Replicant ver.1.22474487139... â a remaster of a game first published in 2010. One reviewer wrote, external, "What we're left with is two nearly identical Niers each vying to be the true Nier."

Fans may react badly should a remaster or remake fail to do justice to the source material. And, in the gaming world, angry fan reactions can be serious.

"There's always going to be that fan base or group of people that are going to nit-pick," says Stephanie Ijoma, chief executive and founder of NNESAGA, a consultancy and creative agency.

Ultimately, games developers working on remakes have to "find the right balance" between appealing to fans and allowing themselves to be creative with the material, she adds. "You can't make art in fear, you can't create in fear."

Ijoma gives the example of Final Fantasy VII. First released in 1997, it got a highly anticipated remake in 2020.

This was a game Ijoma played back in the late 1990s and she was thrilled to be able to share it with younger players today.

First released in 1997, Final Fantasy VII got a remake in 2020

Remakes enable this sharing of experiences because getting old computer hardware or video games consoles to work, while a pastime loved by some, is not possible for everyone.

"I've tried multiple times to play Silent Hill 2 because I'm interested in older games but it's such a difficult game to come into nowadays," says Niklas Hallin, a solo indie game developer who runs the studio Neckbolt. "They made a remake and it's so smooth, it just works. It works on your modern hardware."

Hallin did not play many games as a child so he says nostalgia isn't much of a motivator for him. However, in his own games, he likes to include subtle stylistic references to classic games because he knows that this appeals to gamers.

Indie developers often lean on retro games aesthetics not just because people respond well to them â but also because they are easier to achieve. "You can't compete with billion dollar budgets, super high-fidelity, Triple A graphics," he says.

One question remains. The visual difference, today, between games released years apart is, arguably, negligible. So will there be any point in remaking a game from 2026 in, say, 2038? Will technology have moved on so much by then that it's actually worth it?

Harding-Rolls predicts that games companies will still seek to connect new audiences with classic titles by offering updates.

Remakes might become less about graphical improvements and more about editorial adjustments. "Reinterpretations," he says. "Or versions with additional content."

X-planes: Are they needed in the new era of drones?

The start-ups hoping to return battery making to the US

#154. Meta seeks to raise up to $25 billion in debt sale as AI spending jumps

Meta is seeking to raise between $20 billion and $25 billion in a bond sale on Tuesday as the company ramps up its spending on artificial intelligence infrastructure along with AI models and services.

The debt sale is expected to be two-thirds in U.S. dollars and one-third in euros, according to people familiar with the matter who asked not to be named because the details are private. Final pricing for the bond sale should come Tuesday afternoon, the sources said.

Meta declined to comment. Bloomberg News first reported on Meta's plans.

Meta's bond sale comes days after S&P Global Ratings revised the company's outlook from AA- stable to AA- negative, citing "uncertainty" about its hefty capex spending and negative free cash flow.

In its third-quarter earnings report last month, the Facebook parent revealed that its full-year capex will come in between $135 billion to $145 billion, representing a $5 billion increase to the low end of its previous guidance. Meta posted negative free cash flow of $2.12 billion for its latest quarter, joining Alphabet and Amazon in reporting a negative figure.

Meta previously raised $25 billion in an April bond sale, and said last week that it plans to raise $95 billion in an effort to fund its AI initiatives. Of that, Meta intends to first raise $35 billion in underwritten public equity offerings, and an additional $60 billion through an at-the-market program.

Meta's stock rose 2.7% on Tuesday after dropping in 12 of the prior 14 trading sessions and closing out October with its worst month in four years.

#153. City of London chief tops UK Black Powerlist

Ian Thomas, chief executive of the City of London Corporation, has been named the UK's most influential Black Briton in this year's Black Powerlist awards.

The 57-year old is the first Black person to oversee the Square Mile's governing body in its more than 900-year history.

The City of London Corporation is an administrative body with responsibility for London's central financial district, headed by its own Lord Mayor.

Thomas tops the 2027 Black Powerlist, which recognises the most powerful people of African and Caribbean heritage in the UK.

The award body said he was "a respected public-service leader with over 30 years' experience in local government".

Other influential names in this year's top ten include entrepreneurs Emma Grede and Steven Bartlett, as well as actor and filmmaker Sir Idris Elba.

Comedian Sir Lenny Henry has also been recognised, with a Lifetime Achievement award, for co-founding Comic Relief, which has raised over Â£1.6bn since 1985.

Now in its 21st year, the Black Powerlist celebrates people who have broken through barriers to reach leadership roles, thereby inspiring future generations.

"It exists to show young people of African and Caribbean heritage what is possible, in fields they might never have considered," said Michael Eboda, the list's publisher.

Sir Lenny Henry is being honoured with a Lifetime Achievement Award

Thomas said he secured the role at the head of the City of London Corporation in 2022 only following seven interviews and an election.

'When I started my journey in public service, I never, ever thought, [not] in a million years, that I'd be recognised in this way," he told the BBC.

As chief executive, he oversees around 5,000 staff with responsibilities including the City of London Police, 11,000 acres of open spaces such as Epping Forest and Hampstead Heath, and advocacy for the UK's financial and professional services sector.

Thomas was born in Sheffield to parents who made the city their home after leaving Jamaica as part of the Windrush generation.

"They worked incredibly hard and instilled that sense of discipline and respect, and that's the core of how I do business," he said of his upbringing.

Growing up, Thomas aspired to be a footballer and took part in trials alongside Alan Shearer.

Reflecting on his own change of direction, Thomas joked it was about "following dreams and if they don't come trueâ¦ there's always something else".

Before joining the City of London Corporation, Thomas served as chief executive of the Royal Borough of Kingston upon Thames where he oversaw public services and a Â£1.2bn regeneration programme.

He was appointed a CBE in the 2019 New Year Honours in the wake of the Rotherham Abuse scandal for transforming children's services during his time as strategic director for children and young people at Rotherham Metropolitan Borough Council.

Thomas replaces last year's incumbent, Afua Kyei, the Bank of England's chief financial officer and executive director, and is joined on this year's list by Comfort Ero, the President and chief executive of the International Crisis Group, who takes second place.

#149. US tech stocks see sharp falls as AI spending nears $1tn

Tech stocks tumbled for the second day in a row as concerns rise over the hundreds of billions of dollars firms are investing in artificial intelligence (AI).

Shares in Intel plummeted by 28% on Friday, while Google owner Alphabet saw its stock price continue to fall another 1.4% after a 12% plunge on Thursday.

Other tech stocks, including Reddit and Amazon also fell, while the Nasdaq index of the largest tech firms listed in the US, ended October with its worst monthly slide since March 2025.

While tech firms are spending big on AI, their earnings are not always meeting analysts' expectations, spooking investors and creating fears of an AI bubble.

Major tech companies have ramped up artificial intelligence spending, with Meta, Alphabet, Oracle, Amazon, and Microsoft committing to spending $800bn between them in 2026.

Alphabet's stock market value fell sharply on Thursday, taking more than $500bn (Â£374bn) off its market value after it revealed the AI summaries it now provides as the top answer to search queries were affecting its core search advertising revenue.

At the same time, Alphabet expects to spend up to $205bn this year on investments, almost entirely related to AI.

The bigger losses on Friday were elsewhere however, with Intel shedding more than $130bn in value.

The chip maker said it planned to increase spending on AI despite weaker demand for computers, a major source of demand for its micro-processors.

On Thursday, Intel's finance chief David Zinsner said the firm did not expect to bring in more cash than it spent this year and forecast lower-than-expected revenue for its fourth quarter.

"Looking ahead, we see the PC slowdown continuing at least through the first half of next year," he said in the chip maker's third quarter earnings report.

In the earnings call, he said the firm had significantly ramped up investment in equipment in the third quarter and expected that to be "even more significant" in the following three months.

Amazon on Thursday revealed it had also spent more cash than it was making, and that spending had increased six-fold over the span of three months.

The company has committed to spending $220bn this year, putting the money towards investments such as AI data centres, network equipment, and servers.

Amazon shares recovered some overnight losses, but were still down by 1.4% at the end of Friday trading.

Reddit stock plunged by 29%, after the social media platform revealed weaker-than-expected daily user figures and warned that Google's growing AI-driven search was hurting referral traffic.

Stock in online game platform Roblox also fell, down by 18%, as its daily active user numbers dropped.

Several major tech firms have reported earnings this week, including Microsoft, and analysts said there were questions about the payout from the huge investments in AI.

Chris Low, from investment management firm FHN Financial, said the bulk of what was happening in the stock market was in reaction to some of the biggest tech players such as Amazon and Google.

He told Reuters the companies were "showing some pretty significant growing pains as they aggressively pursue the AI build-out".

"The thinking is that these signs suggest that the entire sector is going to slow down."

Many analysts have been debating the possibility of an AI bubble in the stock market.

The worry is that some tech firms will not be able to justify their sky-high valuations because the cost of building AI data centres and developing the software will end up being greater than the money they can make from it.

Kathleen Brookes, research director at trading firm XTB, said there have been many ups and downs in AI trade in recent months.

"Investors have turned ruthless, punishing any signs of disappointment in [third quarter] earnings," she said.

"The AI trade has had a good run in recent years, but now investors want payback, and companies like Alphabet and Intel are in the crosshairs."

However, stock market values for many of these companies remain well above where they were a year ago.

While the Nasdaq closed 2.8% lower, it is up 7.8% over the last year. Similarly Amazon is up nearly 8% over the past 12 months, while Alphabet is down 1.3%.

Brookes noted that November is typically a strong month for stocks.

"If we see AI stocks continue to struggle in the coming days, it could suggest the AI trade is past its peak, and investors will be looking for a new theme to drive markets," she said.

#147. Baked by Melissa enters Target with first at-home cupcake mix

Baked by Melissa will enter the at-home baking aisle in Target stores as the retailer tries to persuade shoppers to spend more at its locations this holiday season.

As of Sunday, Target is exclusively selling the brand's cupcake kits, liners and a cupcake pan, all in the dessert company's signature bite-size portions. The line includes three cupcake flavors: Christmas Tie-Dye, Marshmallow Hot Cocoa and Red Velvet.

The company's entry into at-home baking comes as a number of restaurants turn to consumer packaged goods to boost sales in an otherwise challenging time for the industry.

Some players, such as California Pizza Kitchen and TGI Fridays, have licensed their brands for frozen foods for decades. But other restaurant chains, including Chick-fil-A and Dutch Bros, have leaned into grocery products much more recently. For smaller companies, such as Momofuku or Carbone, space on a grocery shelf helps extend brand reach and gives consumers a chance to try their food without a reservation and at a much lower cost.

Founded by Melissa Ben-Ishay in 2008, Baked by Melissa has nine permanent locations in the New York area and ships its minicupcakes nationwide.

The regional chain has garnered attention outside the city thanks to Ben-Ishay's social media presence. On TikTok, the Baked by Melissa account has more than 2.8 million followers, fueled by popular recipe videos, such as for her green goddess salad dressing. Ben-Ishay's social media stardom has led to collaborations with Ferrero and Wonder.

Baked by Melissa had discussed at-home baking products for years, but Ben-Ishay, who is also president of the company, only decided to take the plunge a year ago during a trip to Target with her two kids, she told CNBC. A spin through the baking aisle made her decide Baked by Melissa "should be here," she said.

"I love baking cake mix. I truly do. And, quite frankly, sometimes I just think it tastes better," Ben-Ishay said.

But retail moves more slowly than Baked by Melissa's usual pace.

The dessert company needed just a few days to create New York Knicks-branded minicupcakes after the team won the NBA championship in June, for example, but figuring out retail products meant "a lot of waiting and approvals and new partners," Ben-Ishay said.

Twelve months later, the cupcake mixes have arrived on store shelves, just in time for the all-important holiday season.

For Target, the next two months will be critical as the retailer tries to woo back its core customer after a disappointing holiday season last year. That prototypical Target shopper often looks a lot like Ben-Ishay.

"We started with Target because I'm a 42-year-old mom of two, and I find myself there a lot," she said.

#146. Why North Korea's 'elite' hackers are the prime suspect in major Binance hack

Two major blockchain analytics firms say they have robust intelligence pointing to a North Korean state-backed group as the likely culprits in the $6.3bn (Â£4.7bn) hack of Binance, the world's largest cryptocurrency exchange.

The Lazarus Group, a North Korean hacking unit also known as TraderTraitor, was identified "with high confidence" by TRM Labs, as well as Chainalysis.

This incident is just the latest linked to the country, one of the poorest in the world and where access to the internet is severely restricted.

Experts accuse the government of North Korean leader Kim Jong Un of relying on cybercrime to finance itself, something Pyongyang has long denied.

Though not financial, the nation's most spectacular suspected hack took place in 2014. The target: Sony Pictures, which had made the Hollywood film The Interview. The film involves a plot to assassinate Kim.

The resulting hack exposed thousands of embarrassing emails exchanged among the studio's top executives.

Who could be behind the Binance hack and what we know so far

Crypto giant Binance attempting to recover $6.3bn stolen in hack linked to North Korea

Last year, the Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI) said North Korea was responsible for the theft of $1.5bn on the Bybit exchange.

In the aftermath of that incident, the agency said TraderTraitor had moved to quickly launder and disperse the stolen assets, converting them to Bitcoin and other digital assets with a goal of eventually turning them into hard currency.

The FBI has broadly accused North Korea of "illicit cybercrime and virtual asset theft activities".

The nation has systematically developed its cyber operations, recruiting the most promising students from universities and channeling them into hacker groups overseen by North Korea's intelligence agency, according to reports.

Some of the cyberwarfare conducted takes place just over the nation's border in China, according to analysis from tech groups.

Watch: What we know about Binance's $6.3bn hack and what to do if you're affected

Much of its hacking activity has centred on monetary gain.

North Korean hackers are also suspected in the theft of $620m worth of cryptocurrency in 2022.

In this most recent heist, North Korean agents are accused of using a special authorisation to turn a Binance hot wallet, which is connected to the internet, into one that moved funds automatically to the attacker.

TRM said it's likely the attacker gained access to Binance's hot wallet signing system as part of the scheme.

Binance has not directly responded to the BBC's queries about the incident's potential links to the area.

The company had initially said it thought an engineer's laptop had been compromised, allowing unauthorised withdrawals, but later said the attack was wider.

"Our subsequent investigation has uncovered additional points of breach," Binance's co-CEO Richard Teng said.

Some 80% of stolen assets have been secured, a Binance spokesperson said on Friday, but the rest remains unaccounted for.

If the accusations are true, the record-making heist shows how powerful suspected North Korean hackers have become â a sign that the tools of the hacking trade have grown sharper.

Experts say the rise of artificial intelligence has also given threat actors new ways to carry out crime, through automation and other means.

In this case, it's plausible that AI was used to infiltrate Binance's systems.

As Chainalysis put it, the attacker used laundering patterns "we have not seen before".

#143. No more washing up: How Airbnb's new house rules have divided hosts and guests

Airbnb host Lisa says her Welsh holiday let was left 'a complete mess' by a group of guests

When Lisa Clewes returned to her Welsh holiday let after a group of guests checked out, she was horrified by the mess.

Just days earlier, she had met the group in person and helped with their luggage.

But after a dinner party, the house was left "in a complete mess" with broken glasses, drinks cans on the floor, pans full of food on the table and fish guts left in the garden.

"It was really unbelievable. Had I had another booking the same day, I would have had to cancel or delay check-in," says Clewes.

She charges a cleaning fee that covers around 75% of the cost of a cleaner and asks guests to empty the fridge, put rubbish in the bins and load the dishwasher.

Clewes says it typically takes cleaners four hours to turn around her property and fears that guests leaving more mess could mean increasing costs

But Airbnb's new rules mean hosts can no longer ask guests to do jobs normally carried out by cleaners, including washing and putting away dishes, stripping beds and taking rubbish off site.

The updated guidelines, which apply to bookings made globally from 20 November, state that hosts can now set seven standard house rules and add other "reasonable" requests.

Clewes says it typically takes cleaners four hours to turn around her three-bedroom property. She fears guests leaving more mess could mean hiring extra cleaners and increasing costs â expenses that may ultimately be passed on to her guests.

The changes have come as a relief to guests like Jessica Newhook, who says she has been faced with lengthy checkout lists, often on top of hefty cleaning fees.

She booked an Airbnb in Florida for a family reunion of 11 guests and says they had to "leave the property move-in ready for the cleaners", despite paying a $250 (Â£187) cleaning fee.

Newhook says the host told them to wash and fold towels, clean the bedding and kitchen, and sweep and mop the floors. She was also warned by the host that their behaviour needed to improve or they could be told to leave, because they had left chairs blocking bins.

"We had rules about everything and it was not relaxing at all."

While Newhook likes the home comforts of staying in an Airbnb, she says the combination of relentless rules and rising cleaning fees has taken away much of its appeal.

"It is getting to the point where a hotel is cheaper and I know what to expect."

Krissi Foskett, 46, from Glasgow, runs a national cleaning company and regularly stays in Airbnb properties so "can see the issue from both sides".

Krissi says there's a difference between being a respectful guest and being expected to help run the business you've just paid to stay in

During a recent stay in Stafford, she paid a Â£35 cleaning fee but was still asked to strip the beds, put on a wash, empty bins and carry rubbish down a long track to a recycling area.

"I don't particularly mind doing those things, but it did make me question what exactly I was paying the additional cleaning fee for," she says.

For Foskett, there is a difference between being a considerate guest â "putting your dirty dishes in the dishwasher" â and being "expected to prepare the property for the next guests".

Through her cleaning business, she has also seen the other extreme, with teams arriving to find broken furniture, vomit and pet urine.

While she sympathises with hosts facing tight turnarounds and rising cleaning costs, these should not be passed on to guests, she says.

"If you're running an Airbnb as a business, professional cleaning is a cost of doing business."

Anthony Marsh, who co-owns a cabin retreat in North Yorkshire with his husband, used to list five of their 12 properties on Airbnb, but says the new rules have prompted him to reduce this number to just two.

His concern with the changes is that "Airbnb has effectively given guests permission to come and leave properties a tip, and a host can't do anything about it".

Anthony and his husband used to list five of their 12 cabins in North Yorkshire on Airbnb...

... but will now list just two in light of the new rules

He believes asking guests to wash their pots and put out the bins is hardly unreasonable and, with a small cleaning team and tight turnaround times, he worries extra mess could leave properties unready for the next arrivals.

"It's common sense and common decency," he says.

His company does not charge a cleaning fee, and Marsh says most guests are respectful. But fees are more common than not. Some 63.2% of Airbnb listings charge a cleaning fee, with an average cost of Â£55 according to data from AirROI, external.

Laura Lewis runs a directory that helps holiday-let owners market their properties and connect with potential guests.

Many of her clients also list on Airbnb and she says most are unhappy with the new rules, arguing that asking guests to wash up is a matter of basic respect.

"Some people say it's factored into the cleaning fee, but I wouldn't leave a holiday let with all the washing-up to do. It's not the right thing," she says.

Laura Lewis says it's a matter of "basic respect" to wash dishes and take the bins out

The announcement has added to growing frustration among owners following increases in Airbnb's commission rates last year. Many are investing in their own websites and social media to attract direct bookings and avoid platform fees.

Lewis has taken the same approach with the three treehouses on her farm â most of her bookings are already direct and she now plans to remove her listings from Airbnb altogether.

Not all hosts are concerned about the changes, however. Matt Porsz, who hosts and manages other Airbnb properties full-time, believes the update will improve the guest experience without changing how most people behave.

Guests at his property in south-west London are asked only to lock up and return the keys. "People sometimes strip the beds or do things without me asking, and that's nice for my cleaners, but I don't expect it."

Porsz doubts the changes will lead to guests leaving properties in a worse state, pointing to Airbnb's mutual review system.

"If I put a big chore list, I wouldn't expect a five-star review and vice versa â if a guest leaves the place in a state, you're not going to give them a good review."

He believes the system encourages both sides to behave reasonably, and that most guests will continue to treat properties with respect.

"Airbnb is trying to cut out the silly stuff because it's not a good guest experience."

#142. Don't get caught out by new Buy Now Pay Later rules

If you've bought something via Buy Now Pay Later (BNPL) since the summer, then you'll have been tested to see if you can afford it.

BNPL has always been a type of loan, usually paid back in instalments, but it's only been officially regulated since July.

This means stricter background checks on your ability to repay.

If you fail, then your purchase via BNPL will be blocked. If there's a chance of this happening to you, here are some ways to approach the situation.

BNPL lenders - such as Klarna, Clearpay and others - must now have regulator approval to operate, giving shoppers more access to refunds and independent rulings on complaints if things go wrong.

But new affordability checks on each BNPL transaction could also leave you blocked from services for the first time.

There are different affordability checks for different purchases, partially based on how much is borrowed.

But, with the expensive festive season approaching, here's how to avoid being blocked when time and finances are tight.

Here are four things to consider if you find your account is closed or your borrowing limit has been massively reduced, from Sara Williams, a former volunteer debt adviser at Citizens Advice who posts tips as Debt Camel on social media.

Slow down your purchases: is what you are buying essential or can you postpone it?

Be more careful about what you buy to avoid needing a high credit limit to, for example, buy lots of clothes in different sizes

Cancel subscriptions to escape the trap of having to use BNPL to make up for the cost of previous payments

Talk to debt charities for help and access to food bank vouchers if you are using BNPL for essentials

If you are struggling to make repayments on any type of loan - BNPL or otherwise - you should seek help before things spiral out of control.

The government-backed, independent, MoneyHelper website, external has a guide to finding free debt advice near you, external.

It also suggests writing down exactly what you owe, to help plan how you will repay the money.

There are also tools to work out if you are receiving all the benefits and grants you are entitled to, and a bill prioritiser, external to show which payments to make first and what to do if you miss a payment.

You should also be sceptical of supposed debt solutions you might see on your social feeds.

#140. Virgin Media O2 set to cut thousands of jobs to reduce costs

Thousands of Virgin Media O2 staff are facing redundancy as the telecoms firm attempts to save money in the face of billions of pounds of debt and tougher competition.

The broadband provider has not finalised the number of roles to be cut but it is likely be a sizeable chunk of its 16,000-strong workforce, the BBC understands.

Virgin Media O2 has billions in debt on its balance sheet and is tackling both smaller providers as well as giants like BT, which has agreed a deal to acquire rival TalkTalk, which Virgin Media O2 has firmly opposed.

The company declined to comment. Prospect, a union which represents Virgin Media O2 staff, said the cuts were "disappointing and very unsettling".

Noel McClean. deputy general secretary at Prospect, said the union was "committed to supporting our members through what is a difficult time, as well as also working with employers to explore ways to mitigate the number of redundancies and ensure retraining and redeployment of staff affected".

The majority of the affected staff are in roles that are not customer-facing and a number of senior leadership changes are already under way, the BBC has been told.

Virgin Media O2 is also expected to review its use of contractors and suppliers in conjunction with the job cuts.

As of 30 June, Virgin Media O2's total liabilities stands at Â£27.2bn while it holds assets worth Â£45.9bn, according to the firm's latest accounts.

Meanwhile, the company is dealing with fierce competition from cheaper "altnets" like Hyperoptic, Community Fibre, Gigaclear and YouFibre.

Virgin Media O2 had attempted to respond to this growing threat by buying altnet competitor Netomnia for Â£2bn via nexfibre, a joint venture owned by Virgin Media O2's owners.

However, the Competition Markets Authority (CMA) has provisionally found competition concerns in the deal, external, with a final decision to come in December, sparking anger from Virgin Media O2.

In October, Virgin Media O2 called the BT's agreed takeover of TalkTalk a "stitch-up" that had allowed its rival to "tighten its grip" over the market.

BT said its deal provided "a safety net" for TalkTalk customers as their broadband provider faced collapse without the takeover.

On Friday, BT's share price jumped 7% after the Financial Times reported Virgin Media O2 was close to agreeing a deal, external to use its rival's fibre broadband network.

The BBC has been told the deal was very close and that Virgin Media O2 was highly likely to announce it will stop all investment in fibre in its results on Tuesday.

#139. Fannie Mae, Freddie Mac rally as Ackman presses for conservatorship exit

The midterm elections could be a turning point for the release of Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac from government control, according to billionaire investor Bill Ackman.

Investors may agree with him, if the rally in both stocks is any sign.

The Federal National Mortgage Association, known as Fannie Mae, and Federal Home Loan Mortgage Corporation, or Freddie Mac, have each jumped more than 60% from Oct. 14 to Monday's close. The stocks were both up roughly 3% on Tuesday.

Ackman, the founder of Pershing Square Capital Management, has long pushed for the two entities to be released from federal conservatorship.

"The reason why it's not being touched right now is, you know, midterms," he said in an interview with CNBC's "Squawk Box."

Ackman, who owns both names through Pershing Square, believes Freddie and Fannie have the potential to increase "10-fold from current share prices," he said in his quarterly letter to shareholders, issued on Monday.

Fannie and Freddie buy mortgages from banks and other lenders, and then package them into mortgage-backed securities, known as MBS. Their operations are critical to the housing market, providing liquidity to banks and creating a secondary market for MBS.

The entities were placed into conservatorship in September 2008, during the financial crisis. The stocks were delisted from the New York Stock Exchange in 2010 and have traded over the counter since then.

Ackman wants the conservatorship to end, arguing the entities would be worth multiples of their current valuation if they were freed from government control.

President Donald Trump has supported ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie. But it remains politically and economically contentious.

The entities operate under strict capital threshold requirements and would need to be recapitalized, which could happen through issuing common equity. However, the Treasury Department would need to figure out an exit plan for its roughly $388 billion ownership stake in the entities through preferred securities and warrants.

Congress may also need to play a role, passing legislation to restructure the government's role in mortgage financing and home-loan guarantees.

Nonetheless, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent recently said it could happen before Trump's term ends, telling 2Way's "The Morning Meeting" podcast that the administration will tackle the ownership structure "next year, the year after." He said affordability will be guiding the decision.

One point of controversy is whether ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie would increase the spreads on MBS over Treasuries, effectively making mortgages costlier. Bessent has sought to reassure the market that wouldn't happen.

"Whatever we do is going to be guided by making sure that the spread on [agency] mortgages … over the Treasury does not increase," Bessent said.

Ackman argues that privatizing the GSEs would help insulate them from political meddling. "Fannie and Freddie are these massive, enormously profitable institutions where whichever political party controls them…if they're still in conservatorship, can influence the outcome of elections," he told CNBC.

In one scenario, he explained, Democrats win the White House, conservatorship is still in place, and Democrats push Fannie and Freddie to lower credit standards in order to expand home ownership and affordability.

"If they're going to move more aggressively into borrowers with lower credit quality, for example, to achieve a political objective, that's going to have an impact on trillions of dollars outstanding mortgage securities," Ackman said. "It's going to have an impact on overall credit spreads."

MBS investors want certainty about how the entities will be run, he noted.

"That only gets resolved in a world in which they exit conservatorship, they become publicly traded companies that are owned by institutional and retail investors," Ackman said. "We set the rules. In conservatorship, the president can do anything."

#137. Binance users withdraw $3.6bn after largest-ever crypto hack

Binance customers withdrew $3.6bn (Â£2.7bn) of assets from the world's biggest cryptocurrency exchange as it reopened on Sunday following what is believed to be the largest hack in history.

The firm suspended all transactions in the wake of the incident, which impacted $6.3bn worth of digital assets.

Major organisations, including the US Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI), have accused North Korea of being responsible for the cyber-attack.

The company said it has covered around 95% of the assets stolen in the breach but the remaining amount may take longer to source. Binance has repeatedly said it would cover all of the losses linked to the attack.

Binance said on X: "Between 06:00 and 15:30 [GMT] on November 1, we processed $4.649 billion in withdrawals and $1.01 billion in deposits, resulting in a net outflow of approximately $3.64 billion."

There was an "initial spike" in withdrawals, which later declined and "are stabilising as expected", it added.

Sunday's net withdrawals account for just over 2% of the $171bn of assets under its management, said Binance.

The firm said last week that it has reserved more than $6.3bn in funds, to deal with âthe fallout of the attack.

Binance also said withdrawals of more than two dozen major cryptocurrencies like Bitcoin and Ethereum have resumed and that more services will resume in phases once security checks are completed.

Funds linked to the Binance hack continue to be laundered, according to Alec Zebrick, senior manager in global services at Chainalysis, which has been working with the firm and governments to get the stolen money back.

"We continue to see funds being laundered quite actively," he told the BBC's Today Programme.

"The attackers are layering these funds across many thousands of addresses, across thousands of deposits," he added.

AI discovered a massive cyber-security flaw - here's what happened

The attack on Binance has been attributed to TraderTraitor, a North Korean cybercrime group also known as the Lazarus Group, which stole a then-record $1.5bn from crypto exchange Bybit in 2025.

The FBI on Saturday published a list of cryptocurrency wallet addresses linked to the group and urged partners in the industry to help by blocking transactions involving them.

The exchange scrambled to urge cryptocurrency issuers to freeze affected assets.

Binance said last week that early checks suggest the incident may have started with a compromised laptop that belonged to an engineer, allowing hackers to find a way to make unauthorised transactions.

The hack hit Binance's global centralised exchange, Binance.com, while its US-facing platform has said it was not affected by the issue.

Watch: What we know about Binance's $6.3bn hack and what to do if you're affected

Binance said it had replaced customers' assets using a combination of sources, including its corporate treasury as well as its futures insurance and emergency reserve funds.

"Our commitment remains the same: Binance will fully cover the financial impact of the funds stolen in this incident," the firm said.

Cryptocurrencies are digital assets traded on exchanges such as Binance. Unlike cash, they do not exist in physical form and are managed on computers, smartphones and other digital devices.

Binance was founded in China and has hundreds of millions of registered users globally.

#135. The next wave of luxury real estate: marina developments with yacht parking

Marinas are becoming the next big draw for luxury real estate, as the global wealthy search for homes to park their ever-larger yachts, according to developers.

With the number of yachts surging since Covid-19, real estate developers and investors are rushing to fill the shortage of boat slips and marina communities. There are now over 6,000 superyachts over 30 meters (98 feet) around the world, and another 200 delivered every year, according to SuperYacht Times.

The growth in the size and number of yachts has created a shortage of yacht slips. And investors are rushing to fill demand. Last year, Blackstone paid $5.6 billion for Safe Harbor Marinas, which then paid $1.5 billion in August for 65 marinas and storage sites purchased from MarineMax. European private equity firms are also on a marina buying spree to fill the shortage in the Mediterranean.

Real estate developers now see marinas as a powerful magnet for ultra-wealthy buyers, especially in South Florida. Billionaire Ken Griffin is building a megayacht marina in Miami with eight megayacht slips, including one for his 308-foot yacht.

The largest and most ambitious is Related Group's $2 billion new marina development in Fort Lauderdale. Slated to break ground next year and open in 2030, the project will include a St. Regis Hotel, condos, a beach club and slips for yachts up to 350 feet long.

"Here in South Florida is there's been a scarcity of yachting destinations and marinas and slips," said Related Group's Nick Pérez. "So when you're able to combine irreplaceable real estate and a marina, you just have a winning formula."

The 40-acre development will include a St. Regis hotel with 122 rooms, a 78-residence condo tower, 106 resort residences, as well as a deepwater marina that can hold 250 boats. It will also include a sports and wellness club with a padel and tennis court, yoga lawn, fitness center and pools. And it will have its own beach club.

Fort Lauderdale has long been the yachting capital of the U.S., with a vast infrastructure of yacht repair and renovation companies, along with crew, brokers, charter experts and shipbuilder offices. An estimated 2,000 superyachts pass through Fort Lauderdale each year, according to tourism officials. Many yacht owners are looking for real estate not only for themselves but also their crew.

Pérez said the new St. Regis development will not only appeal to the yachting community but also to a growing number of international buyers who are looking for a lower-cost alternative to Palm Beach and Miami. Several buyers from Mexico and Brazil have already purchased in the building.

He said up to 30% of the buyers at the project could be international.

"Fort Lauderdale is no longer T-shirt shops and flip-flops, it's now a luxury destination, with national retailers like Rolex, Patek, Cartier," Pérez said. "When you look at Miami and Palm Beach and the growth, now it's Fort Lauderdale's time to shine."

While boat slips are the main attraction, many buyers in Fort Lauderdale are pouring in from Southern California and the Northeast to take advantage of Florida's lower taxes.

"With the taxation situation in California, we're seeing a lot of interest from Newport and the Laguna area and San Diego," Pérez said.

The Inside Wealth newsletter by Robert Frank is your weekly guide to high-net-worth investors and the industries that serve them.

#132. Poundland's former owner offers to invest as minister prepares to meet board

Poundland has been given until Thursday to find a rescue deal, putting up to 12,000 staff at risk.

Poundland's founder has said he would invest in the firm and a government minister said she would meet the firm's board amid its administration fears.

Steve Smith, who set-up the Walsall-based business, said he had attempted to contact owners Gordon Brothers having been "really surprised" to learn about them filing for administration when sales were up 11% on last year.

The BBC understands that Business Minister Kate Dearden will meet with senior Poundland executives later this week to discuss the company's threatened insolvency.

US-based Gordon Brothers said it was evaluating interest for the whole business and formal and indicative offers for significant viable parts of it.

Gordon Brothers, who bought the firm from Polish retail group Pepco last year for Â£1, has stopped funding the business and rejected a takeover offer from Irish firm Martin Property Group (MPG).

The retailer has given the business until Thursday to find a rescue deal, putting up to 12,000 staff at risk.

Steven Smith founded Poundland in 1990 with the help of his father, Kevin Smith

It's understood the meeting with Deardon will be predominantly a "fact finding" meeting with government sources conceding there is limited scope for the government to intervene in a commercial transaction albeit one that puts over 10,000 jobs and hundreds of stores at risk.

The meeting was first reported by Sky News.

There is no current meeting planned between the government and executives from current owners, US private equity group Gordon Brothers.

Concerns have been raised that Gordon Brothers have declined an offer from another buyer to keep Poundland afloat in order to ensure repayment of Â£30 million loan it inherited after buying the company for Â£1.

'Poundland closure would leave a void in our town'

How Poundland grew from a market stall to a high street giant

Poundland staff protest after chain approach administrators

Prime Minister Andy Burnham has said the government was "ready to support" staff as he also called for answers over why an offer to buy the business had been rejected.

Smith, from Wolverhampton and who set up the firm in 1990 with a store in Burton-upon-Trent, Staffordshire, said he contacted the owners over the last three months but had no reply. He had thought that was good news as he assumed the firm must have had offers on the table.

"And maybe I was a bit too late, but I'd certainly... with talking to the staff and how Poundland's performing at the moment, I'd certainly, if there's an opportunity, I definitely want to put some money back in there."

He said he was still in touch with staff there at levels within the business and "even last week" sales were 11.2 % upon last year.

"Although Poundland made a loss last year, they're going to make a good profit this year, so it's just crazy."

Staff held a protest outside Poundland's Walsall-based HQ last week

He warned that the two or three thousand suppliers to the firm only worked for Poundland so they would be directly affected by its collapse.

"This is not just about 12, 000 jobs. I'm estimating more like 20 or 30,000 jobs," he added.

The company has 600 stores in the UK and Ireland, and Smith said it had between four and five million customers a week, so with Poundland not being present on high streets would "cause a lot of other retailers to fall as well".

Liam Byrne, chair of the Business, Innovation, Science and Trade Committee and Labour MP for Birmingham Hodge Hill and Solihull North, said he felt the company could be saved and "the whole deal stinks to high heaven right now".

"Because I think the issue that we've got is, you've got an owner that could well be trying to cut and run, to sell a business that is in fact a going concern in order to maximize their own profits and put in jeopardy 12,000 British jobs on the high street."

"Our message today to government and to ministers is, do not leave this to the last minute, which is Thursday... please get on the case now to get everybody around the table to maximize every option to save as much of the business as possible," he added.

In a statement, the board of Poundland Limited said it had been "working on a formal sale process for Poundland" and despite "enormous efforts it had not been possible to identify an acceptable offer for the business as a whole".

"We recognise any sale process creates uncertainty and particularly when set against a challenging UK economic environment."

Gordon Brothers said: "While Martin Property Group had been identified as the preferred bidder by Poundland's management team, attention must now turn to other active bids."

The interest, formal and indicative offers were being evaluated "with a view to minimising uncertainty for Poundland's people and all relevant stakeholders."

#131. Ferrari raises 2026 guidance after Q3 earnings beat

Ferrari on Tuesday raised its 2026 guidance after the sports car maker slightly beat Wall Street's third-quarter expectations.

Here's how Ferrari performed in the third quarter compared with average estimates compiled by LSEG:

Ferrari raised its guidance to revenue of 7.65 billion euros, up from 7.6 billion euros; adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization to 3 billion euros, up from 2.97 billion euros; and adjusted EPS to at least 9.85 euros, up from at least 9.68 euros.

The automaker also upgraded its expectations for industrial free cash flow to up to 1.65 billion euros, up from previous expectations for at least 1.55 billion euros.

This is the second consecutive quarter Ferrari has raised its 2026 guidance, despite vehicle shipments being lower by 318 units through the first nine months of the year, at 10,170 vehicles, compared with last year. The decline is led by fewer shipments to the Americas and China, Hong Kong and Taiwan.

Ferrari said Tuesday that the guidance improvements were a result of stronger-than-expected customer personalizations, lower than anticipated currency headwinds and a second place finish in the Formula 1 championship.

"Our order book now reaches into 2028, and we still have two more launches to come this year. In addition, we continue to create engaging events around the world to further tighten the bonds with our clients and partners across Racing, Sports Cars and Lifestyle," Ferrari CEO Benedetto Vigna said in a release.

RBC Capital Markets analyst Tom Narayan said Tuesday that Ferrari's results were largely in line with Wall Street expectations. However, he said shares may be "under slight pressure given some were hoping for a bigger guidance raise, but expect it to get better once folks realize these results are healthy."

Ferrari's U.S. stock initially declined on the results before turning positive to close Tuesday at $414.77 a share, up 2.9%.

#130. Ryanair says it may freeze workers' pay and raise airfares

Ryanair is considering a temporary wage freeze for staff and confirmed airfares will be "materially higher" next summer because of a sharp rise in fuel costs following the US-Israel war with Iran.

The airline said it expects fuel prices to remain high "well into 2027" unless there is a "swift resolution" to the conflict.

Ryanair said its fuel bill is forecast to be â¬2bn (Â£1.6bn) higher in the short-term and its boss, Michael O'Leary, said airfares would have to rise by "double-digits" to help cover the cost.

But the firm said that was unlikely to be enough and it is looking into pay freezes. Unite, the union representing Ryanair staff, said it had not been told of "any such proposals".

O'Leary also said the company would slow down hiring as airline's costs rise.

The airline said its pre-tax profits fell by 16% to â¬2.4bn during the six months to the end of September, largely due to fuel costs jumping by 15%.

The company said that although 80% of its fuel bill had been "hedged" â in effect, bought in advance at fixed prices â the cost of the remainder was elevated.

O'Leary reiterated forecasts from earlier this year Ryanair would "need a 20% fare increase" to cover the cost of jet fuel.

"I'm not sure we'll get to 20%," he said. "But we're certainly looking at double-digit fare increases across Europe from all airlines in 2027."

Aviation fuel prices have surged this year as a result of the US-Israel war with Iran, which has dramatically reduced the supply of refined products coming from the Gulf and increased competition for those produced elsewhere.

Ryanair also said it was weighing up whether to freeze pay, as multi-year wage deals with unions come to to an end. The policy could be implemented from April next year.

Oliver Richardson, national officer at Unite, said the union "hasn't been advised of any such proposals and we have a multi-year pay deal in place which covers next year. A number of Unions across Europe have the same".

He said: "Our pay agreement was not attached to, nor dependent on company performance. This was something that the Ryanair was at pains to emphasise. As such we would expect it to be honoured."

Ryanair also said it would increase its use of artificial intelligence within its back office and support functions.

Meanwhile, Ryanair has cut back on winter flights and said on Monday there would be more to come. It said this was happening across the airline industry, particularly with short-haul carriers.

O'Leary said: "We believe those cuts will continue into the summer of 2027. Over the medium term, we expect short-haul capacity will remain constrained out to the early 2030s."

#129. Sen. Chris Murphy still hospitalized after suffering a stroke over the weekend

Sen. Chris Murphy remained hospitalized Tuesday after suffering a stroke over the weekend, his office confirmed.

"He's continuing to improve and heartened by the outpouring of well wishes," Josh Sorbe, Murphy's communications director, told CNBC in an emailed statement. "He early voted in Connecticut, and he encourages everyone to make their voices heard today by voting and sending a message in this defining election."

His office did not offer additional details when asked about his status or his expected recovery.

The Senate is due back in session Monday following Tuesday's midterm elections.

Murphy began experiencing stroke symptoms on Saturday while at his son's hockey game, his chief of staff Allison Herwitt said in a statement Sunday.

"He was taken to a local hospital, where it was discovered he had experienced a stroke. He was transferred to Johns Hopkins for further monitoring and is responding well to treatment," Herwitt said.

Herwitt said at the time that Murphy was "awake and alert."

Johns Hopkins Hospital's main campus is in Baltimore, though it has other locations in and around Washington.

Murphy is not up for reelection on Tuesday but had been traveling extensively this fall in support of Democratic candidates. Murphy came to the Senate in 2013 after a six-year stint in the U.S. House. He's also been mentioned as a potential 2028 presidential candidate.

#128. Christmas 2026 stamps unveiled by Royal Mail

Two stamps from the Royal Mail's annual Christmas collection

Royal Mail has unveiled its Christmas stamps for this year - featuring scenes from the Nativity.

The series of five stamps have been jointly created by graphic designer Gareth Howat and illustrator Rebecca Sutherland.

The stamps depict familiar scenes of Mary and Joseph travelling, baby Jesus, an angel, the shepherds and the Three Wise Men.

This year's collection marks the 60th anniversary of the first-ever Christmas special stamp.

Sutherland said it was an "absolute honour" to be given the opportunity to illustrate the Christmas stamps for the Royal Mail.

She said she has loved stamps since she was a child and had even collected them. "Being asked to create a set myself feels incredibly special," she added.

The Christmas stamps from recent years have featured snowy scenes of UK cathedrals, or lyrics from famous carols - although 2025 also showed scenes from the Nativity.

The first Christmas stamp collection came out in December 1966 after a children's art competition was announced by the then-postmaster Tony Benn.

David Gold, of Royal Mail, said: "We hope this year's Christmas stamps will add an extra touch of festive magic to the millions of greetings sent to family and friends at home and around the world."
